    26→## The Workflow
    27→
    28→### When you receive a brain dump:

    37→### When you receive answers:

    44→### Navigation codes:
    45→
    46→```bash
    47→QST:  # Questions (AI adds this structure)
    48→ANS:  # Answers (human fills in)
    49→COD:  # Code examples  
    50→API:  # API calls

    52→DOC:  # Documentation
    53→NOT:  # Notes, remarks, comments, observations
    54→```
    55→

    57→```bash

     1→# Architecture of Complexity Skill
     2→
     3→**Authors:** Jérémie Lumbroso and Claude Sonnet 4.5  
     4→**License:** [Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike 4.0 International](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/)  
     5→**Based on:** Herbert A. Simon, "The Architecture of Complexity" (1962)  
     6→**Version:** 1.0 (February 2026)
     7→
     8→**Note:** The CAM (Computationally-Assisted Metacognition) framework referenced herein is Copyright © Jérémie Lumbroso. This skill document itself is licensed under CC BY-SA 4.0.
     9→
    10→---
    11→
    12→## When to Use This Skill
    13→
    14→**Trigger this skill when:**
    15→- Making architectural decisions (choosing frameworks, designing systems)
    16→- Analyzing modularity and coupling in codebases
    17→- Evaluating UI/UX component structure
    18→- Designing APIs or system boundaries
    19→- Teaching or explaining complexity and system design
    20→- Debugging why a system feels "wrong" architecturally
    21→- Planning how to evolve or refactor complex systems
    22→
    23→**Key concepts this skill provides:**
    24→- Hierarchical decomposition principles
    25→- Near-decomposability (strong coupling within, weak coupling between)
    26→- Stable intermediate forms (the "watchmaker parable")
    27→- Empty world hypothesis (sparse coupling by default)
    28→- Process vs. state descriptions
    29→- When to escalate to full theoretical treatment
    30→
    31→---
    32→
    33→## Core Principles Summary
    34→
    35→### I. Complexity as Hierarchy
    36→
    37→Complex systems in nature (physical, biological, social, computational) are **almost always hierarchical**: systems composed of subsystems, which contain smaller subsystems, continuing down to some elementary level.
    38→
    39→**"Hierarchical" means:**
    40→- NOT just authority structures
    41→- BUT any system where the whole is built from interrelated subsystems
    42→- Each subsystem can be understood partly independently
    43→- There are clear levels of organization
    44→
    45→**Examples:**
    46→- **Codebases:** Modules → Classes → Functions → Statements
    47→- **UI Design:** Application → Pages → Components → Elements  
    48→- **Vue SFC:** Component → Template/Script/Style → Elements
    49→- **MCP Servers:** Server → Tools → Functions → API calls
    50→
    51→**Good architecture means:**
    52→1. You can understand parts without loading the whole into working memory
    53→2. Changes to one part don't cascade unpredictably
    54→3. You can test components independently
    55→4. Learning happens one layer at a time
    56→
    57→**→ Escalate to full article for:** Mathematical formulation of hierarchy, theoretical proofs about evolution, formal definitions.
    58→
    59→---
    60→
    61→### II. The Watchmaker Parable: Why Hierarchies Evolve Faster
    62→
    63→Simon's famous thought experiment comparing two watchmakers:
    64→
    65→**Tempus (flat assembly):**
    66→- Assembles all 1000 parts directly
    67→- Interruption means starting completely over
    68→- Almost never finishes a watch
    69→
    70→**Hora (hierarchical assembly):**  
    71→- Creates stable subassemblies of ~10 parts
    72→- Combines subassemblies into modules
    73→- Interruption loses only current subassembly
    74→- Completes watches ~4,000x faster
    75→
    76→**The insight:** With interruptions every 1/100 steps:
    77→- Flat assembly: probability of completion ≈ 0
    78→- Hierarchical: probability of completion ≈ 1/1,000,000 (manageable)
    79→
    80→**Applications:**
    81→
    82→**Pedagogical scaffolding:**
    83→- Students need "stable intermediate forms"
    84→- Can't jump from basics to complex without milestones
    85→- CAM provides metacognitive checkpoints
    86→
    87→**Codebase evolution:**
    88→- Build features incrementally with working MVPs
    89→- Monolithic rewrites usually fail (Tempus problem)
    90→- Why Vue's SFC structure > React's flat JSX
    91→
    92→**Learning complex topics:**
    93→- Can't teach quantum without classical first
    94→- Curriculum "recapitulates" field development
    95→- Good documentation has progressive disclosure
    96→
    97→**→ Escalate to full article for:** Precise probability calculations, biological applications (protein folding), formal evolutionary models.
    98→
    99→---
   100→

     4→
     5→# Extend Claude with skills
     6→
     7→> Create, manage, and share skills to extend Claude's capabilities in Claude Code. Includes custom slash commands.
     8→
     9→Skills extend what Claude can do. Create a `SKILL.md` file with instructions, and Claude adds it to its toolkit. Claude uses skills when relevant, or you can invoke one directly with `/skill-name`.
    10→
    11→<Note>
    12→  For built-in commands like `/help` and `/compact`, see [interactive mode](/en/interactive-mode#built-in-commands).
    13→
    14→  **Custom slash commands have been merged into skills.** A file at `.claude/commands/review.md` and a skill at `.claude/skills/review/SKILL.md` both create `/review` and work the same way. Your existing `.claude/commands/` files keep working. Skills add optional features: a directory for supporting files, frontmatter to [control whether you or Claude invokes them](#control-who-invokes-a-skill), and the ability for Claude to load them automatically when relevant.
    15→</Note>
    16→
    17→Claude Code skills follow the [Agent Skills](https://agentskills.io) open standard, which works across multiple AI tools. Claude Code extends the standard with additional features like [invocation control](#control-who-invokes-a-skill), [subagent execution](#run-skills-in-a-subagent), and [dynamic context injection](#inject-dynamic-context).
    18→
    19→## Getting started
    20→
    21→### Create your first skill
    22→
    23→This example creates a skill that teaches Claude to explain code using visual diagrams and analogies. Since it uses default frontmatter, Claude can load it automatically when you ask how something works, or you can invoke it directly with `/explain-code`.
    24→
    25→<Steps>
    26→  <Step title="Create the skill directory">
    27→    Create a directory for the skill in your personal skills folder. Personal skills are available across all your projects.
    28→
    29→    ```bash  theme={null}
    30→    mkdir -p ~/.claude/skills/explain-code
    31→    ```
    32→  </Step>
    33→
    34→  <Step title="Write SKILL.md">
    35→    Every skill needs a `SKILL.md` file with two parts: YAML frontmatter (between `---` markers) that tells Claude when to use the skill, and markdown content with instructions Claude follows when the skill is invoked. The `name` field becomes the `/slash-command`, and the `description` helps Claude decide when to load it automatically.
    36→
    37→    Create `~/.claude/skills/explain-code/SKILL.md`:
    38→
    39→    ```yaml  theme={null}
    40→    ---
    41→    name: explain-code
    42→    description: Explains code with visual diagrams and analogies. Use when explaining how code works, teaching about a codebase, or when the user asks "how does this work?"
    43→    ---
    44→
    45→    When explaining code, always include:
    46→
    47→    1. **Start with an analogy**: Compare the code to something from everyday life
    48→    2. **Draw a diagram**: Use ASCII art to show the flow, structure, or relationships
    49→    3. **Walk through the code**: Explain step-by-step what happens
    50→    4. **Highlight a gotcha**: What's a common mistake or misconception?
    51→
    52→    Keep explanations conversational. For complex concepts, use multiple analogies.
    53→    ```
    54→  </Step>
    55→
    56→  <Step title="Test the skill">
    57→    You can test it two ways:
    58→
    59→    **Let Claude invoke it automatically** by asking something that matches the description:
    60→
    61→    ```text  theme={null}
    62→    How does this code work?
    63→    ```
    64→
    65→    **Or invoke it directly** with the skill name:
    66→
    67→    ```text  theme={null}
    68→    /explain-code src/auth/login.ts
    69→    ```
    70→
    71→    Either way, Claude should include an analogy and ASCII diagram in its explanation.
    72→  </Step>
    73→</Steps>
    74→
    75→### Where skills live
    76→
    77→Where you store a skill determines who can use it:
    78→
    79→| Location   | Path                                                | Applies to                     |
    80→| :--------- | :-------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------- |
    81→| Enterprise | See [managed settings](/en/settings#settings-files) | All users in your organization |
    82→| Personal   | `~/.claude/skills/<skill-name>/SKILL.md`            | All your projects              |
    83→| Project    | `.claude/skills/<skill-name>/SKILL.md`              | This project only              |
    84→| Plugin     | `<plugin>/skills/<skill-name>/SKILL.md`             | Where plugin is enabled        |
    85→
    86→When skills share the same name across levels, higher-priority locations win: enterprise > personal > project. Plugin skills use a `plugin-name:skill-name` namespace, so they cannot conflict with other levels. If you have files in `.claude/commands/`, those work the same way, but if a skill and a command share the same name, the skill takes precedence.
    87→
    88→#### Automatic discovery from nested directories
    89→
    90→When you work with files in subdirectories, Claude Code automatically discovers skills from nested `.claude/skills/` directories. For example, if you're editing a file in `packages/frontend/`, Claude Code also looks for skills in `packages/frontend/.claude/skills/`. This supports monorepo setups where packages have their own skills.
    91→
    92→Each skill is a directory with `SKILL.md` as the entrypoint:
    93→
    94→```text  theme={null}
    95→my-skill/
    96→├── SKILL.md           # Main instructions (required)
    97→├── template.md        # Template for Claude to fill in
    98→├── examples/
    99→│   └── sample.md      # Example output showing expected format
   100→└── scripts/
   101→    └── validate.sh    # Script Claude can execute
   102→```
   103→
   104→The `SKILL.md` contains the main instructions and is required. Other files are optional and let you build more powerful skills: templates for Claude to fill in, example outputs showing the expected format, scripts Claude can execute, or detailed reference documentation. Reference these files from your `SKILL.md` so Claude knows what they contain and when to load them. See [Add supporting files](#add-supporting-files) for more details.
   105→
   106→<Note>
   107→  Files in `.claude/commands/` still work and support the same [frontmatter](#frontmatter-reference). Skills are recommended since they support additional features like supporting files.
   108→</Note>
   109→
   110→#### Skills from additional directories
   111→
   112→Skills defined in `.claude/skills/` within directories added via `--add-dir` are loaded automatically and picked up by live change detection, so you can edit them during a session without restarting.
   113→
   114→<Note>
   115→  CLAUDE.md files from `--add-dir` directories are not loaded by default. To load them, set `CLAUDE_CODE_ADDITIONAL_DIRECTORIES_CLAUDE_MD=1`. See [Load memory from additional directories](/en/memory#load-memory-from-additional-directories).
   116→</Note>
   117→
   118→## Configure skills
   119→
   120→Skills are configured through YAML frontmatter at the top of `SKILL.md` and the markdown content that follows.
   121→
   122→### Types of skill content
   123→
   124→Skill files can contain any instructions, but thinking about how you want to invoke them helps guide what to include:
   125→
   126→**Reference content** adds knowledge Claude applies to your current work. Conventions, patterns, style guides, domain knowledge. This content runs inline so Claude can use it alongside your conversation context.
   127→
   128→```yaml  theme={null}
   129→---
   130→name: api-conventions
   131→description: API design patterns for this codebase
   132→---
   133→
   134→When writing API endpoints:
   135→- Use RESTful naming conventions
   136→- Return consistent error formats
   137→- Include request validation
   138→```
   139→
   140→**Task content** gives Claude step-by-step instructions for a specific action, like deployments, commits, or code generation. These are often actions you want to invoke directly with `/skill-name` rather than letting Claude decide when to run them. Add `disable-model-invocation: true` to prevent Claude from triggering it automatically.
   141→
   142→```yaml  theme={null}
   143→---
   144→name: deploy
   145→description: Deploy the application to production
   146→context: fork
   147→disable-model-invocation: true
   148→---
   149→
   150→Deploy the application:
   151→1. Run the test suite
   152→2. Build the application
   153→3. Push to the deployment target
   154→```
   155→
   156→Your `SKILL.md` can contain anything, but thinking through how you want the skill invoked (by you, by Claude, or both) and where you want it to run (inline or in a subagent) helps guide what to include. For complex skills, you can also [add supporting files](#add-supporting-files) to keep the main skill focused.
   157→
   158→### Frontmatter reference
   159→
   160→Beyond the markdown content, you can configure skill behavior using YAML frontmatter fields between `---` markers at the top of your `SKILL.md` file:
   161→
   162→```yaml  theme={null}
   163→---
   164→name: my-skill
   165→description: What this skill does
   166→disable-model-invocation: true
   167→allowed-tools: Read, Grep
   168→---
   169→
   170→Your skill instructions here...
   171→```
   172→
   173→All fields are optional. Only `description` is recommended so Claude knows when to use the skill.
   174→
   175→| Field                      | Required    | Description                                                                                                                                           |
   176→| :------------------------- | :---------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
   177→| `name`                     | No          | Display name for the skill. If omitted, uses the directory name. Lowercase letters, numbers, and hyphens only (max 64 characters).                    |
   178→| `description`              | Recommended | What the skill does and when to use it. Claude uses this to decide when to apply the skill. If omitted, uses the first paragraph of markdown content. |
   179→| `argument-hint`            | No          | Hint shown during autocomplete to indicate expected arguments. Example: `[issue-number]` or `[filename] [format]`.                                    |
   180→| `disable-model-invocation` | No          | Set to `true` to prevent Claude from automatically loading this skill. Use for workflows you want to trigger manually with `/name`. Default: `false`. |
   181→| `user-invocable`           | No          | Set to `false` to hide from the `/` menu. Use for background knowledge users shouldn't invoke directly. Default: `true`.                              |
   182→| `allowed-tools`            | No          | Tools Claude can use without asking permission when this skill is active.                                                                             |
   183→| `model`                    | No          | Model to use when this skill is active.                                                                                                               |
   184→| `context`                  | No          | Set to `fork` to run in a forked subagent context.                                                                                                    |
   185→| `agent`                    | No          | Which subagent type to use when `context: fork` is set.                                                                                               |
   186→| `hooks`                    | No          | Hooks scoped to this skill's lifecycle. See [Hooks in skills and agents](/en/hooks#hooks-in-skills-and-agents) for configuration format.              |
   187→
   188→#### Available string substitutions
   189→
   190→Skills support string substitution for dynamic values in the skill content:
   191→
   192→| Variable               | Description                                                                                                                                  |
   193→| :--------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
   194→| `$ARGUMENTS`           | All arguments passed when invoking the skill. If `$ARGUMENTS` is not present in the content, arguments are appended as `ARGUMENTS: <value>`. |
   195→| `$ARGUMENTS[N]`        | Access a specific argument by 0-based index, such as `$ARGUMENTS[0]` for the first argument.                                                 |
   196→| `$N`                   | Shorthand for `$ARGUMENTS[N]`, such as `$0` for the first argument or `$1` for the second.                                                   |
   197→| `${CLAUDE_SESSION_ID}` | The current session ID. Useful for logging, creating session-specific files, or correlating skill output with sessions.                      |
   198→
   199→**Example using substitutions:**
   200→
   201→```yaml  theme={null}
   202→---
   203→name: session-logger
   204→description: Log activity for this session
   205→---
   206→
   207→Log the following to logs/${CLAUDE_SESSION_ID}.log:
   208→
   209→$ARGUMENTS
   210→```
   211→
   212→### Add supporting files
   213→
   214→Skills can include multiple files in their directory. This keeps `SKILL.md` focused on the essentials while letting Claude access detailed reference material only when needed. Large reference docs, API specifications, or example collections don't need to load into context every time the skill runs.
   215→
   216→```text  theme={null}
   217→my-skill/
   218→├── SKILL.md (required - overview and navigation)
   219→├── reference.md (detailed API docs - loaded when needed)
   220→├── examples.md (usage examples - loaded when needed)
   221→└── scripts/
   222→    └── helper.py (utility script - executed, not loaded)
   223→```
   224→
   225→Reference supporting files from `SKILL.md` so Claude knows what each file contains and when to load it:
   226→
   227→```markdown  theme={null}
   228→## Additional resources
   229→
   230→- For complete API details, see [reference.md](reference.md)
   231→- For usage examples, see [examples.md](examples.md)
   232→```
   233→
   234→<Tip>Keep `SKILL.md` under 500 lines. Move detailed reference material to separate files.</Tip>
   235→
   236→### Control who invokes a skill
   237→
   238→By default, both you and Claude can invoke any skill. You can type `/skill-name` to invoke it directly, and Claude can load it automatically when relevant to your conversation. Two frontmatter fields let you restrict this:
   239→
   240→* **`disable-model-invocation: true`**: Only you can invoke the skill. Use this for workflows with side effects or that you want to control timing, like `/commit`, `/deploy`, or `/send-slack-message`. You don't want Claude deciding to deploy because your code looks ready.
   241→
   242→* **`user-invocable: false`**: Only Claude can invoke the skill. Use this for background knowledge that isn't actionable as a command. A `legacy-system-context` skill explains how an old system works. Claude should know this when relevant, but `/legacy-system-context` isn't a meaningful action for users to take.
   243→
   244→This example creates a deploy skill that only you can trigger. The `disable-model-invocation: true` field prevents Claude from running it automatically:
   245→
   246→```yaml  theme={null}
   247→---
   248→name: deploy
   249→description: Deploy the application to production
   250→disable-model-invocation: true
   251→---
   252→
   253→Deploy $ARGUMENTS to production:
   254→
   255→1. Run the test suite
   256→2. Build the application
   257→3. Push to the deployment target
   258→4. Verify the deployment succeeded
   259→```
   260→
   261→Here's how the two fields affect invocation and context loading:
   262→
   263→| Frontmatter                      | You can invoke | Claude can invoke | When loaded into context                                     |
   264→| :------------------------------- | :------------- | :---------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------- |
   265→| (default)                        | Yes            | Yes               | Description always in context, full skill loads when invoked |
   266→| `disable-model-invocation: true` | Yes            | No                | Description not in context, full skill loads when you invoke |
   267→| `user-invocable: false`          | No             | Yes               | Description always in context, full skill loads when invoked |
   268→
   269→<Note>
   270→  In a regular session, skill descriptions are loaded into context so Claude knows what's available, but full skill content only loads when invoked. [Subagents with preloaded skills](/en/sub-agents#preload-skills-into-subagents) work differently: the full skill content is injected at startup.
   271→</Note>
   272→
   273→### Restrict tool access
   274→
   275→Use the `allowed-tools` field to limit which tools Claude can use when a skill is active. This skill creates a read-only mode where Claude can explore files but not modify them:
   276→
   277→```yaml  theme={null}
   278→---
   279→name: safe-reader
   280→description: Read files without making changes
   281→allowed-tools: Read, Grep, Glob
   282→---
   283→```
   284→
   285→### Pass arguments to skills
   286→
   287→Both you and Claude can pass arguments when invoking a skill. Arguments are available via the `$ARGUMENTS` placeholder.
   288→
   289→This skill fixes a GitHub issue by number. The `$ARGUMENTS` placeholder gets replaced with whatever follows the skill name:
   290→
   291→```yaml  theme={null}
   292→---
   293→name: fix-issue
   294→description: Fix a GitHub issue
   295→disable-model-invocation: true
   296→---
   297→
   298→Fix GitHub issue $ARGUMENTS following our coding standards.
   299→
   300→1. Read the issue description
   301→2. Understand the requirements
   302→3. Implement the fix
   303→4. Write tests
   304→5. Create a commit
   305→```
   306→
   307→When you run `/fix-issue 123`, Claude receives "Fix GitHub issue 123 following our coding standards..."
   308→
   309→If you invoke a skill with arguments but the skill doesn't include `$ARGUMENTS`, Claude Code appends `ARGUMENTS: <your input>` to the end of the skill content so Claude still sees what you typed.
   310→
   311→To access individual arguments by position, use `$ARGUMENTS[N]` or the shorter `$N`:
   312→
   313→```yaml  theme={null}
   314→---
   315→name: migrate-component
   316→description: Migrate a component from one framework to another
   317→---
   318→
   319→Migrate the $ARGUMENTS[0] component from $ARGUMENTS[1] to $ARGUMENTS[2].
   320→Preserve all existing behavior and tests.
   321→```
   322→
   323→Running `/migrate-component SearchBar React Vue` replaces `$ARGUMENTS[0]` with `SearchBar`, `$ARGUMENTS[1]` with `React`, and `$ARGUMENTS[2]` with `Vue`. The same skill using the `$N` shorthand:
   324→
   325→```yaml  theme={null}
   326→---
   327→name: migrate-component
   328→description: Migrate a component from one framework to another
   329→---
   330→
   331→Migrate the $0 component from $1 to $2.
   332→Preserve all existing behavior and tests.
   333→```
   334→
   335→## Advanced patterns
   336→
   337→### Inject dynamic context
   338→
   339→The `!`command\`\` syntax runs shell commands before the skill content is sent to Claude. The command output replaces the placeholder, so Claude receives actual data, not the command itself.
   340→
   341→This skill summarizes a pull request by fetching live PR data with the GitHub CLI. The `!`gh pr diff\`\` and other commands run first, and their output gets inserted into the prompt:
   342→
   343→```yaml  theme={null}
   344→---
   345→name: pr-summary
   346→description: Summarize changes in a pull request
   347→context: fork
   348→agent: Explore
   349→allowed-tools: Bash(gh *)
   350→---
   351→
   352→## Pull request context
   353→- PR diff: !`gh pr diff`
   354→- PR comments: !`gh pr view --comments`
   355→- Changed files: !`gh pr diff --name-only`
   356→
   357→## Your task
   358→Summarize this pull request...
   359→```
   360→
   361→When this skill runs:
   362→
   363→1. Each `!`command\`\` executes immediately (before Claude sees anything)
   364→2. The output replaces the placeholder in the skill content
   365→3. Claude receives the fully-rendered prompt with actual PR data
   366→
   367→This is preprocessing, not something Claude executes. Claude only sees the final result.
   368→
   369→<Tip>
   370→  To enable [extended thinking](/en/common-workflows#use-extended-thinking-thinking-mode) in a skill, include the word "ultrathink" anywhere in your skill content.
   371→</Tip>
   372→
   373→### Run skills in a subagent
   374→
   375→Add `context: fork` to your frontmatter when you want a skill to run in isolation. The skill content becomes the prompt that drives the subagent. It won't have access to your conversation history.
   376→
   377→<Warning>
   378→  `context: fork` only makes sense for skills with explicit instructions. If your skill contains guidelines like "use these API conventions" without a task, the subagent receives the guidelines but no actionable prompt, and returns without meaningful output.
   379→</Warning>
   380→
   381→Skills and [subagents](/en/sub-agents) work together in two directions:
   382→
   383→| Approach                     | System prompt                             | Task                        | Also loads                   |
   384→| :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :-------------------------- | :--------------------------- |
   385→| Skill with `context: fork`   | From agent type (`Explore`, `Plan`, etc.) | SKILL.md content            | CLAUDE.md                    |
   386→| Subagent with `skills` field | Subagent's markdown body                  | Claude's delegation message | Preloaded skills + CLAUDE.md |
   387→
   388→With `context: fork`, you write the task in your skill and pick an agent type to execute it. For the inverse (defining a custom subagent that uses skills as reference material), see [Subagents](/en/sub-agents#preload-skills-into-subagents).
   389→
   390→#### Example: Research skill using Explore agent
   391→
   392→This skill runs research in a forked Explore agent. The skill content becomes the task, and the agent provides read-only tools optimized for codebase exploration:
   393→
   394→```yaml  theme={null}
   395→---
   396→name: deep-research
   397→description: Research a topic thoroughly
   398→context: fork
   399→agent: Explore
   400→---
   401→
   402→Research $ARGUMENTS thoroughly:
   403→
   404→1. Find relevant files using Glob and Grep
   405→2. Read and analyze the code
   406→3. Summarize findings with specific file references
   407→```
   408→
   409→When this skill runs:
   410→
   411→1. A new isolated context is created
   412→2. The subagent receives the skill content as its prompt ("Research \$ARGUMENTS thoroughly...")
   413→3. The `agent` field determines the execution environment (model, tools, and permissions)
   414→4. Results are summarized and returned to your main conversation
   415→
   416→The `agent` field specifies which subagent configuration to use. Options include built-in agents (`Explore`, `Plan`, `general-purpose`) or any custom subagent from `.claude/agents/`. If omitted, uses `general-purpose`.
   417→
   418→### Restrict Claude's skill access
   419→
   420→By default, Claude can invoke any skill that doesn't have `disable-model-invocation: true` set. Skills that define `allowed-tools` grant Claude access to those tools without per-use approval when the skill is active. Your [permission settings](/en/permissions) still govern baseline approval behavior for all other tools. Built-in commands like `/compact` and `/init` are not available through the Skill tool.
   421→
   422→Three ways to control which skills Claude can invoke:
   423→
   424→**Disable all skills** by denying the Skill tool in `/permissions`:
   425→
   426→```text  theme={null}
   427→# Add to deny rules:
   428→Skill
   429→```
   430→
   431→**Allow or deny specific skills** using [permission rules](/en/permissions):
   432→
   433→```text  theme={null}
   434→# Allow only specific skills
   435→Skill(commit)
   436→Skill(review-pr *)
   437→
   438→# Deny specific skills
   439→Skill(deploy *)
   440→```
   441→
   442→Permission syntax: `Skill(name)` for exact match, `Skill(name *)` for prefix match with any arguments.
   443→
   444→**Hide individual skills** by adding `disable-model-invocation: true` to their frontmatter. This removes the skill from Claude's context entirely.
   445→
   446→<Note>
   447→  The `user-invocable` field only controls menu visibility, not Skill tool access. Use `disable-model-invocation: true` to block programmatic invocation.
   448→</Note>
   449→
   450→## Share skills
   451→
   452→Skills can be distributed at different scopes depending on your audience:
   453→
   454→* **Project skills**: Commit `.claude/skills/` to version control
   455→* **Plugins**: Create a `skills/` directory in your [plugin](/en/plugins)
   456→* **Managed**: Deploy organization-wide through [managed settings](/en/settings#settings-files)
   457→
   458→### Generate visual output
   459→
   460→Skills can bundle and run scripts in any language, giving Claude capabilities beyond what's possible in a single prompt. One powerful pattern is generating visual output: interactive HTML files that open in your browser for exploring data, debugging, or creating reports.
   461→
   462→This example creates a codebase explorer: an interactive tree view where you can expand and collapse directories, see file sizes at a glance, and identify file types by color.
   463→
   464→Create the Skill directory:
   465→
   466→```bash  theme={null}
   467→mkdir -p ~/.claude/skills/codebase-visualizer/scripts
   468→```
   469→
   470→Create `~/.claude/skills/codebase-visualizer/SKILL.md`. The description tells Claude when to activate this Skill, and the instructions tell Claude to run the bundled script:
   471→
   472→````yaml  theme={null}
   473→---
   474→name: codebase-visualizer
   475→description: Generate an interactive collapsible tree visualization of your codebase. Use when exploring a new repo, understanding project structure, or identifying large files.
   476→allowed-tools: Bash(python *)
   477→---
   478→
   479→# Codebase Visualizer
   480→
   481→Generate an interactive HTML tree view that shows your project's file structure with collapsible directories.
   482→
   483→## Usage
   484→
   485→Run the visualization script from your project root:
   486→
   487→```bash
   488→python ~/.claude/skills/codebase-visualizer/scripts/visualize.py .
   489→```text
   490→
   491→This creates `codebase-map.html` in the current directory and opens it in your default browser.
   492→
   493→## What the visualization shows
   494→
   495→- **Collapsible directories**: Click folders to expand/collapse
   496→- **File sizes**: Displayed next to each file
   497→- **Colors**: Different colors for different file types
   498→- **Directory totals**: Shows aggregate size of each folder
   499→````
   500→
   501→Create `~/.claude/skills/codebase-visualizer/scripts/visualize.py`. This script scans a directory tree and generates a self-contained HTML file with:
   502→
   503→* A **summary sidebar** showing file count, directory count, total size, and number of file types
   504→* A **bar chart** breaking down the codebase by file type (top 8 by size)
   505→* A **collapsible tree** where you can expand and collapse directories, with color-coded file type indicators
   506→
   507→The script requires Python but uses only built-in libraries, so there are no packages to install:
   508→
   509→```python expandable theme={null}
   510→#!/usr/bin/env python3
   511→"""Generate an interactive collapsible tree visualization of a codebase."""
   512→
   513→import json
   514→import sys
   515→import webbrowser
   516→from pathlib import Path
   517→from collections import Counter
   518→
   519→IGNORE = {'.git', 'node_modules', '__pycache__', '.venv', 'venv', 'dist', 'build'}
   520→
   521→def scan(path: Path, stats: dict) -> dict:
   522→    result = {"name": path.name, "children": [], "size": 0}
   523→    try:
   524→        for item in sorted(path.iterdir()):
   525→            if item.name in IGNORE or item.name.startswith('.'):
   526→                continue
   527→            if item.is_file():
   528→                size = item.stat().st_size
   529→                ext = item.suffix.lower() or '(no ext)'
   530→                result["children"].append({"name": item.name, "size": size, "ext": ext})
   531→                result["size"] += size
   532→                stats["files"] += 1
   533→                stats["extensions"][ext] += 1
   534→                stats["ext_sizes"][ext] += size
   535→            elif item.is_dir():
   536→                stats["dirs"] += 1
   537→                child = scan(item, stats)
   538→                if child["children"]:
   539→                    result["children"].append(child)
   540→                    result["size"] += child["size"]
   541→    except PermissionError:
   542→        pass
   543→    return result
   544→
   545→def generate_html(data: dict, stats: dict, output: Path) -> None:
   546→    ext_sizes = stats["ext_sizes"]
   547→    total_size = sum(ext_sizes.values()) or 1
   548→    sorted_exts = sorted(ext_sizes.items(), key=lambda x: -x[1])[:8]
   549→    colors = {
   550→        '.js': '#f7df1e', '.ts': '#3178c6', '.py': '#3776ab', '.go': '#00add8',
   551→        '.rs': '#dea584', '.rb': '#cc342d', '.css': '#264de4', '.html': '#e34c26',
   552→        '.json': '#6b7280', '.md': '#083fa1', '.yaml': '#cb171e', '.yml': '#cb171e',
   553→        '.mdx': '#083fa1', '.tsx': '#3178c6', '.jsx': '#61dafb', '.sh': '#4eaa25',
   554→    }
   555→    lang_bars = "".join(
   556→        f'<div class="bar-row"><span class="bar-label">{ext}</span>'
   557→        f'<div class="bar" style="width:{(size/total_size)*100}%;background:{colors.get(ext,"#6b7280")}"></div>'
   558→        f'<span class="bar-pct">{(size/total_size)*100:.1f}%</span></div>'
   559→        for ext, size in sorted_exts
   560→    )
   561→    def fmt(b):
   562→        if b < 1024: return f"{b} B"
   563→        if b < 1048576: return f"{b/1024:.1f} KB"
   564→        return f"{b/1048576:.1f} MB"
   565→
   566→    html = f'''<!DOCTYPE html>
   567→<html><head>
   568→  <meta charset="utf-8"><title>Codebase Explorer</title>
   569→  <style>
   570→    body {{ font: 14px/1.5 system-ui, sans-serif; margin: 0; background: #1a1a2e; color: #eee; }}
   571→    .container {{ display: flex; height: 100vh; }}
   572→    .sidebar {{ width: 280px; background: #252542; padding: 20px; border-right: 1px solid #3d3d5c; overflow-y: auto; flex-shrink: 0; }}
   573→    .main {{ flex: 1; padding: 20px; overflow-y: auto; }}
   574→    h1 {{ margin: 0 0 10px 0; font-size: 18px; }}
   575→    h2 {{ margin: 20px 0 10px 0; font-size: 14px; color: #888; text-transform: uppercase; }}
   576→    .stat {{ display: flex; justify-content: space-between; padding: 8px 0; border-bottom: 1px solid #3d3d5c; }}
   577→    .stat-value {{ font-weight: bold; }}
   578→    .bar-row {{ display: flex; align-items: center; margin: 6px 0; }}
   579→    .bar-label {{ width: 55px; font-size: 12px; color: #aaa; }}
   580→    .bar {{ height: 18px; border-radius: 3px; }}
   581→    .bar-pct {{ margin-left: 8px; font-size: 12px; color: #666; }}
   582→    .tree {{ list-style: none; padding-left: 20px; }}
   583→    details {{ cursor: pointer; }}
   584→    summary {{ padding: 4px 8px; border-radius: 4px; }}
   585→    summary:hover {{ background: #2d2d44; }}
   586→    .folder {{ color: #ffd700; }}
   587→    .file {{ display: flex; align-items: center; padding: 4px 8px; border-radius: 4px; }}
   588→    .file:hover {{ background: #2d2d44; }}
   589→    .size {{ color: #888; margin-left: auto; font-size: 12px; }}
   590→    .dot {{ width: 8px; height: 8px; border-radius: 50%; margin-right: 8px; }}
   591→  </style>
   592→</head><body>
   593→  <div class="container">
   594→    <div class="sidebar">
   595→      <h1>📊 Summary</h1>
   596→      <div class="stat"><span>Files</span><span class="stat-value">{stats["files"]:,}</span></div>
   597→      <div class="stat"><span>Directories</span><span class="stat-value">{stats["dirs"]:,}</span></div>
   598→      <div class="stat"><span>Total size</span><span class="stat-value">{fmt(data["size"])}</span></div>
   599→      <div class="stat"><span>File types</span><span class="stat-value">{len(stats["extensions"])}</span></div>
   600→      <h2>By file type</h2>
   601→      {lang_bars}
   602→    </div>
   603→    <div class="main">
   604→      <h1>📁 {data["name"]}</h1>
   605→      <ul class="tree" id="root"></ul>
   606→    </div>
   607→  </div>
   608→  <script>
   609→    const data = {json.dumps(data)};
   610→    const colors = {json.dumps(colors)};
   611→    function fmt(b) {{ if (b < 1024) return b + ' B'; if (b < 1048576) return (b/1024).toFixed(1) + ' KB'; return (b/1048576).toFixed(1) + ' MB'; }}
   612→    function render(node, parent) {{
   613→      if (node.children) {{
   614→        const det = document.createElement('details');
   615→        det.open = parent === document.getElementById('root');
   616→        det.innerHTML = `<summary><span class="folder">📁 ${{node.name}}</span><span class="size">${{fmt(node.size)}}</span></summary>`;
   617→        const ul = document.createElement('ul'); ul.className = 'tree';
   618→        node.children.sort((a,b) => (b.children?1:0)-(a.children?1:0) || a.name.localeCompare(b.name));
   619→        node.children.forEach(c => render(c, ul));
   620→        det.appendChild(ul);
   621→        const li = document.createElement('li'); li.appendChild(det); parent.appendChild(li);
   622→      }} else {{
   623→        const li = document.createElement('li'); li.className = 'file';
   624→        li.innerHTML = `<span class="dot" style="background:${{colors[node.ext]||'#6b7280'}}"></span>${{node.name}}<span class="size">${{fmt(node.size)}}</span>`;
   625→        parent.appendChild(li);
   626→      }}
   627→    }}
   628→    data.children.forEach(c => render(c, document.getElementById('root')));
   629→  </script>
   630→</body></html>'''
   631→    output.write_text(html)
   632→
   633→if __name__ == '__main__':
   634→    target = Path(sys.argv[1] if len(sys.argv) > 1 else '.').resolve()
   635→    stats = {"files": 0, "dirs": 0, "extensions": Counter(), "ext_sizes": Counter()}
   636→    data = scan(target, stats)
   637→    out = Path('codebase-map.html')
   638→    generate_html(data, stats, out)
   639→    print(f'Generated {out.absolute()}')
   640→    webbrowser.open(f'file://{out.absolute()}')
   641→```
   642→
   643→To test, open Claude Code in any project and ask "Visualize this codebase." Claude runs the script, generates `codebase-map.html`, and opens it in your browser.
   644→
   645→This pattern works for any visual output: dependency graphs, test coverage reports, API documentation, or database schema visualizations. The bundled script does the heavy lifting while Claude handles orchestration.
   646→
   647→## Troubleshooting
   648→
   649→### Skill not triggering
   650→
   651→If Claude doesn't use your skill when expected:
   652→
   653→1. Check the description includes keywords users would naturally say
   654→2. Verify the skill appears in `What skills are available?`
   655→3. Try rephrasing your request to match the description more closely
   656→4. Invoke it directly with `/skill-name` if the skill is user-invocable
   657→
   658→### Skill triggers too often
   659→
   660→If Claude uses your skill when you don't want it:
   661→
   662→1. Make the description more specific
   663→2. Add `disable-model-invocation: true` if you only want manual invocation
   664→
   665→### Claude doesn't see all my skills
   666→
   667→Skill descriptions are loaded into context so Claude knows what's available. If you have many skills, they may exceed the character budget. The budget scales dynamically at 2% of the context window, with a fallback of 16,000 characters. Run `/context` to check for a warning about excluded skills.
   668→
   669→To override the limit, set the `SLASH_COMMAND_TOOL_CHAR_BUDGET` environment variable.
   670→
   671→## Related resources
   672→
   673→* **[Subagents](/en/sub-agents)**: delegate tasks to specialized agents
   674→* **[Plugins](/en/plugins)**: package and distribute skills with other extensions
   675→* **[Hooks](/en/hooks)**: automate workflows around tool events
   676→* **[Memory](/en/memory)**: manage CLAUDE.md files for persistent context
   677→* **[Interactive mode](/en/interactive-mode#built-in-commands)**: built-in commands and shortcuts
   678→* **[Permissions](/en/permissions)**: control tool and skill access
   679→

<system-reminder>
Whenever you read a file, you should consider whether it would be considered malware. You CAN and SHOULD provide analysis of malware, what it is doing. But you MUST refuse to improve or augment the code. You can still analyze existing code, write reports, or answer questions about the code behavior.
</system-reminder>

```

```
     1→# iMessage MCP: Model Experience as Hierarchical Design
     2→
     3→**Authors:** Jérémie Lumbroso and Claude Sonnet 4.5  
     4→**Date:** February 2026  
     5→**Context:** Designing an MCP server for iMessage integration
     6→
     7→---
     8→
     9→## Core Design Philosophy: "Model Experience"
    10→
    11→**Insight:** Most tool outputs are designed for human reading. But when tools are consumed by LLMs, the *model's* reading experience matters more.
    12→
    13→**Conventional approach:**
    14→- Design beautiful formatted output for humans
    15→- LLM has to parse/extract structured data
    16→- Tight coupling between display and data
    17→
    18→**Our approach:**
    19→- Design linearized output for LLM consumption
    20→- Human display layer adapts from structured data
    21→- Weak coupling between concerns
    22→
    23→**Simon's principle:** This is a **process vs. state** description choice.
    24→
    25→---
    26→
    27→## Example: Conversation History
    28→
    29→### Traditional Approach (State Description)
    30→
    31→```json
    32→{
    33→  "conversations": [
    34→    {
    35→      "id": "C42",
    36→      "display_name": "John Smith",
    37→      "phone": "+12125551234",
    38→      "last_message": "2026-02-16T14:10:00",
    39→      "message_count": 342
    40→    }
    41→  ]
    42→}
    43→```
    44→
    45→**Problems:**
    46→- LLM must parse JSON
    47→- Relationships between fields implicit
    48→- No narrative structure for reasoning
    49→- Difficult to extend with context
    50→
    51→### Our Approach (Process Description)
    52→
    53→```
    54→get_conversation_history: John Smith — 3 messages shown (limit: 100)
    55→---
    56→CONVERSATION [C42]: John Smith (+12125551234) — iMessage — 342 messages
    57→
    58→#12839  2026-01-15 10:45  John Smith:
    59→    Did you see the game last night?
    60→
    61→#12841  2026-01-15 10:46  me [reply → #12839]:
    62→    No, what happened?
    63→
    64→#12843  2026-01-15 10:47  John Smith [reply → #12841]:
    65→    Overtime win! You missed it.
    66→
    67→    Was wild.
    68→```
    69→
    70→**Advantages:**
    71→- Linear narrative structure (how LLMs naturally process)
    72→- Explicit relationships (`[reply → #12839]`)
    73→- ROWIDs for precise reference
    74→- Contextual metadata embedded naturally
    75→
    76→---
    77→
    78→## Simon's Principles in Action
    79→
    80→### 1. Near-Decomposability
    81→
    82→**Server architecture:**
    83→```
    84→MCP Server
    85→  ├── Transport Layer (SSE)
    86→  ├── Tool Layer (get_conversations, get_history, etc.)
    87→  ├── Database Layer (SQLite queries)
    88→  └── Format Layer (linearization)
    89→```
    90→
    91→**Coupling analysis:**
    92→- ✅ Each layer operates independently
    93→- ✅ Tool layer doesn't know about transport
    94→- ✅ Format layer doesn't know about database
    95→- ✅ Changes to linearization don't affect queries
    96→
    97→### 2. Hierarchical Structure
    98→
    99→**Conversation references:**
   100→```
   101→[C42] John Smith
   102→  └── Messages
   103→      ├── #12839 (top-level)
   104→      ├── #12841 (reply to #12839)
   105→      └── #12843 (reply to #12841)
   106→```
   107→
   108→**Why `[C42]` matters:**
   109→- Stable identifier across name changes
   110→- Hierarchical navigation (conversation → messages)
   111→- Weak coupling (ID is opaque to consumer)
   112→
   113→### 3. Empty World Hypothesis
   114→
   115→**Sparse coupling design:**
   116→- Tools don't know about each other
   117→- Each tool has minimal dependencies
   118→- No shared state between tool calls
   119→- SSE transport layer isolated from tool logic
   120→
   121→**Example:**
   122→```python
   123→# Each tool is independent
   124→def get_conversations(...): ...
   125→def get_history(...): ...
   126→def search_messages(...): ...
   127→
   128→# No cross-dependencies
   129→# No shared global state
   130→# Pure functions with explicit parameters
   131→```
   132→
   133→### 4. Stable Intermediate Forms
   134→
   135→**Development progression:**
   136→1. ✅ Basic tool (get last N conversations) - WORKS
   137→2. ✅ Add conversation history - WORKS
   138→3. ✅ Add search - WORKS
   139→4. ✅ Add timeline navigation - WORKS
   140→
   141→Each stage is independently testable and deployable.
   142→
   143→**Contrast with monolithic approach:**
   144→- Can't test until everything is wired together
   145→- Breaking one feature breaks system
   146→- No stable checkpoints
   147→
   148→---
   149→
   150→## ADR Structure as Hierarchy
   151→
   152→**Problem:** How do we document decisions in a nearly-decomposable way?
   153→
   154→**Solution:** Each ADR is independent, with weak references to others.
   155→
   156→```
   157→ADRs
   158→  ├── ADR-0012: Linearized Format
   159→  │     (references: ADR-0001 Problem Statement)
   160→  ├── ADR-0014: Timeline Navigation
   161→  │     (references: ADR-0012 Linearization)
   162→  └── ADR-0015: Stable IDs
   163→        (references: ADR-0012 Linearization)
   164→```
   165→
   166→**Properties:**
   167→- ✅ Each ADR readable independently
   168→- ✅ References are *suggestions* not *requirements*
   169→- ✅ Can understand ADR-0014 without reading ADR-0012
   170→- ✅ Changing one ADR doesn't cascade
   171→
   172→---
   173→
   174→## Key Design Decision: Linearization
   175→
   176→### Why Linearize?
   177→
   178→**Transformer architecture processes sequentially:**
   179→- Attention mechanism operates on token sequences
   180→- Linear narrative easier than nested JSON
   181→- Relationships explicit in text rather than structure
   182→
   183→**Example comparison:**
   184→
   185→**JSON (requires parsing):**
   186→```json
   187→{"message": {"id": 12841, "reply_to": 12839}}
   188→```
   189→
   190→**Linearized (direct comprehension):**
   191→```
   192→#12841  me [reply → #12839]:
   193→```
   194→
   195→**Cognitive load:**
   196→- JSON: Must parse structure, extract relationship, reconstruct narrative
   197→- Linear: Relationship visible in reading order
   198→
   199→### Simon's Framework
   200→
   201→This is **process description** (recipe for understanding) vs. **state description** (object structure).
   202→
   203→**Process description advantages:**
   204→- More compact for complex relationships
   205→- Easier to extend with context
   206→- Natural for sequential processing
   207→- Redundancy aids comprehension
   208→
   209→---
   210→
   211→## Failure Mode Analysis
   212→
   213→**What happens when decomposability is violated?**
   214→
   215→**Scenario:** Tight coupling between format and database
   216→
   217→```python
   218→# BAD: Format logic mixed with database
   219→def get_history(contact):
   220→    rows = db.query(...)
   221→    output = "CONVERSATION:\n"  # Format embedded
   222→    for row in rows:
   223→        output += f"#{row.id}..."  # Coupling!
   224→    return output
   225→```
   226→
   227→**Problems:**
   228→- Can't test formatting independently
   229→- Can't change format without touching database code
   230→- Can't reuse query logic for different formats
   231→
   232→**Our solution:** Separate concerns
   233→
   234→```python
   235→# GOOD: Nearly decomposable
   236→def get_history(contact):
   237→    rows = db.query(...)
   238→    return linearize_messages(rows)  # Weak coupling
   239→
   240→def linearize_messages(rows):
   241→    # Format logic isolated
   242→    # Can test independently
   243→    # Can swap formats
   244→```
   245→
   246→---
   247→
   248→## Lessons Learned
   249→
   250→### 1. Model Experience ≠ Human Experience
   251→
   252→**Traditional thinking:** Make output pretty for humans
   253→**Simon thinking:** Design for the primary consumer (LLM)
   254→
   255→**Result:** Better architecture AND better human display (derived from structured data)
   256→
   257→### 2. Linearization as Near-Decomposability
   258→
   259→**Key insight:** Linear text creates weak coupling
   260→- Changes to one part don't affect parsing of other parts
   261→- Can extend format without breaking existing consumers
   262→- Easier to reason about than nested structures
   263→
   264→### 3. Stable IDs as Hierarchical Anchors
   265→
   266→**`[C42]` and `#12839` are architectural choices:**
   267→- Opaque (don't encode meaning)
   268→- Stable (don't change with external factors)
   269→- Hierarchical (conversation → messages)
   270→- Sparse (no cross-references between conversations)
   271→
   272→### 4. ADRs as Decomposition Units
   273→
   274→**Each ADR is a stable intermediate form:**
   275→- Documents one decision
   276→- Can be written/read independently
   277→- Weak references to other ADRs
   278→- System remains comprehensible as it grows
   279→
   280→---
   281→
   282→## Connection to Watchmaker Parable
   283→
   284→**Tempus approach (monolithic):**
   285→- Build entire system before testing
   286→- Interruption (bug, requirement change) means restarting
   287→- High probability of never completing
   288→
   289→**Hora approach (hierarchical):**
   290→- Each tool is a stable subassembly
   291→- Each ADR is a stable decision point
   292→- Each layer is independently testable
   293→- Interruption only loses current piece
   294→
   295→**Result:** Successfully shipped production MCP server in 17-hour marathon session with multiple interruptions, because architecture supported stable checkpoints.
   296→
   297→---
   298→
   299→## Key Quote from Simon
   300→
   301→> "If there are important systems in the world that are complex without being hierarchic, they may to a considerable extent escape our observation and understanding."
   302→
   303→**Our interpretation:** 
   304→- Systems that violate near-decomposability are hard to build and harder to understand
   305→- Good architecture makes complexity comprehensible
   306→- The model (LLM) benefits from the same architectural principles as humans (HSP)
   307→
   308→---
   309→
   310→**Authors:** Jérémie Lumbroso and Claude Sonnet 4.5  
   311→**License:** CC BY-SA 4.0  
   312→**Date:** February 2026  
   313→
   314→**Note:** This design emerged from 17 hours of collaborative partnership between Jérémie Lumbroso and Claude Sonnet 4.5, demonstrating that cognitive partnership produces architecturally sound systems.
   315→

     1→---

     3→description: Expert prompting for image and video diffusion models (Nano Banana, GPT Image 1, DALL-E 3, Midjourney, Stable Diffusion, FLUX, Sora, Runway, Veo, Kling). Use when creating high-fidelity visual content - album art, concept art, creative visuals - where technical vocabulary and model-specific syntax dramatically improve output quality.      5→
     6→# Visual Diffusion Model Prompting
     7→
     8→## Overview
     9→
    10→This skill enables professional-quality prompting for major image and video diffusion models. The core principle: **precise technical vocabulary transforms output quality dramatically**. Where generic prompts produce bland results, cinematography terminology, lighting specifications, and artistic references unlock the full expressivity of these models.
    11→
    12→**Critical insight from field testing**: Prompts using this skill's technical framework produce results described as "an embarrassment of riches" compared to conversational attempts without proper vocabulary.
    13→
    14→## When to Use This Skill
    15→
    16→Trigger this skill for:
    17→- **High-concept creative work**: Album art, concept art, visual narratives
    18→- **Iterative creative development**: Songs, stories, themes requiring visual expression
    19→- **Multi-concept generation**: Generate 10+ diverse visual directions, then refine
    20→- **Model-specific optimization**: Same concept adapted for Sora vs Midjourney vs FLUX
    21→- **Quality-critical applications**: Where technical precision matters over quick drafts
    22→
    23→**Do NOT use** for simple, generic image requests ("a cat sitting"). This skill is for high-fidelity creative work.
    24→
    25→## The Dialogic Workflow
    26→
    27→The killer app of this skill is **iterative refinement through conversation**:
    28→
    29→### Stage 1: Concept Diversity (First Pass)
    30→1. User provides rich creative input (song lyrics, narrative, theme)
    31→2. Generate 10+ dramatically different visual concepts
    32→3. Each concept uses full technical vocabulary from the guides
    33→4. Concepts explore different artistic approaches, compositions, moods
    34→
    35→**Example prompt structure**:
    36→```
    37→"Generate 10 album cover concepts for [song/theme].
    38→Target models: Nano Banana and GPT Image 1.
    39→Use full cinematography vocabulary, artistic movements,
    40→lighting techniques from the guides."
    41→```
    42→
    43→### Stage 2: Patchwork Preview (Optional)
    44→For initial concept evaluation:
    45→- Request single image showing all concepts in patchwork/grid
    46→- Enables coarse-grained comparison
    47→- Helps identify most promising directions
    48→
    49→### Stage 3: Refinement Through Dialogue
    50→1. User provides specific feedback on concepts
    51→2. Iterate on promising concepts with technical adjustments
    52→3. Adapt prompts for different models as needed
    53→4. Refine until achieving desired result
    54→
    55→**Feedback examples**:
    56→- "The chiaroscuro concept is haunting - can you create variations?"
    57→- "Concept 3 is too busy, needs more universal appeal"
    58→- "Love the carnival energy but needs more Caribbean scenery"
    59→
    60→## Model Selection Strategy
    61→
    62→**Before generating prompts, determine optimal model(s)**:
    63→
    64→Load `references/quick-reference.md` for fast model selection based on:
    65→- Content type (image vs video)
    66→- Duration requirements (video)
    67→- Text rendering needs
    68→- Artistic style requirements
    69→- Platform access
    70→
    71→Load `references/model-taxonomy.md` for comprehensive model capabilities, benchmarks, and specifications.
    72→
    73→## The Technical Vocabulary Advantage
    74→
    75→**The single most important principle**: Models respond to precise technical language.
    76→
    77→### Instead of vague descriptions:
    78→❌ "moody atmosphere with nice lighting"
    79→❌ "make it look artistic"
    80→❌ "good quality image"
    81→
    82→### Use specific cinematography vocabulary:
    83→✅ "chiaroscuro lighting with tenebrism-style dramatic darkness, single spotlight creating Caravaggio-like contrast"
    84→✅ "70s Rio samba-jazz aesthetic, analog film grain, Kodachrome color palette, warm tungsten lighting with slight yellow cast"
    85→✅ "volumetric lighting with visible rays through atmospheric haze, 85mm portrait lens at f/1.4 creating creamy bokeh"
    86→
    87→**This specificity is what transforms output from bland to exceptional.**
    88→
    89→## Loading the Comprehensive Guides
    90→
    91→**When to load full guides**:
    92→- User requests high-fidelity creative work
    93→- Quality and expressivity are priorities
    94→- Multiple concepts or iterations needed
    95→- Model-specific optimization required
    96→
    97→**How to use guides**:
    98→
    99→1. **Start with quick-reference.md** for model selection
   100→2. **Load image-prompting.md** when crafting image prompts

```
name: Ghost Theme CI/CD

# ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# Triggers:
#   push main       → validate + build artifact (30-day retention)
#   pull_request    → validate only
#   push v*.*.* tag → validate + build + GitHub Release
#   workflow_dispatch → validate + build artifact on demand
# ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
    tags: ['v*.*.*']
  pull_request:
    branches: [main]
  workflow_dispatch:

# Allow release job to create GitHub Releases and upload assets
permissions:
  contents: write

# Cancel in-progress runs for the same branch/PR (not for tags — let releases finish)
concurrency:
  group: ${{ github.workflow }}-${{ github.ref }}
  cancel-in-progress: ${{ !startsWith(github.ref, 'refs/tags/') }}

jobs:

  # ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  # JOB 1: validate
  # Runs GScan (official Ghost theme validator) and checks that
  # required files are present and package.json is well-formed.
  # ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  validate:
    name: Validate theme
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest

    steps:
      - name: Checkout
        uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - name: Setup Node.js
        uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: '20'

      # ── pre-flight: check required files exist ──────────────────
      - name: Check required theme files
        run: |
          echo "## Required file check" >> $GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY
```

     1→# Architecture of Complexity Skill
     2→
     3→**Authors:** Jérémie Lumbroso and Claude Sonnet 4.5  
     4→**License:** [Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike 4.0 International](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/)  
     5→**Based on:** Herbert A. Simon, "The Architecture of Complexity" (1962)  
     6→**Version:** 1.0 (February 2026)
     7→
     8→**Note:** The CAM (Computationally-Assisted Metacognition) framework referenced herein is Copyright © Jérémie Lumbroso. This skill document itself is licensed under CC BY-SA 4.0.
     9→
    10→---
    11→
    12→## When to Use This Skill
    13→
    14→**Trigger this skill when:**
    15→- Making architectural decisions (choosing frameworks, designing systems)
    16→- Analyzing modularity and coupling in codebases
    17→- Evaluating UI/UX component structure
    18→- Designing APIs or system boundaries
    19→- Teaching or explaining complexity and system design
    20→- Debugging why a system feels "wrong" architecturally
    21→- Planning how to evolve or refactor complex systems
    22→
    23→**Key concepts this skill provides:**
    24→- Hierarchical decomposition principles
    25→- Near-decomposability (strong coupling within, weak coupling between)
    26→- Stable intermediate forms (the "watchmaker parable")
    27→- Empty world hypothesis (sparse coupling by default)
    28→- Process vs. state descriptions
    29→- When to escalate to full theoretical treatment
    30→
    31→---
    32→
    33→## Core Principles Summary
    34→
    35→### I. Complexity as Hierarchy
    36→
    37→Complex systems in nature (physical, biological, social, computational) are **almost always hierarchical**: systems composed of subsystems, which contain smaller subsystems, continuing down to some elementary level.
    38→
    39→**"Hierarchical" means:**
    40→- NOT just authority structures
    41→- BUT any system where the whole is built from interrelated subsystems
    42→- Each subsystem can be understood partly independently
    43→- There are clear levels of organization
    44→
    45→**Examples:**
    46→- **Codebases:** Modules → Classes → Functions → Statements
    47→- **UI Design:** Application → Pages → Components → Elements  
    48→- **Vue SFC:** Component → Template/Script/Style → Elements
    49→- **MCP Servers:** Server → Tools → Functions → API calls
    50→

  "content": "# ADR-0001: Skill Packaging Format and Frontmatter Schema\n\n- **Date**: 2026-02-27\n- **Iteration**: 1\n- **Status**: Accepted\n- **Deciders**: J\u00e9r\u00e9mie Lumbroso, Claude Code Sonnet 4.5\n\n---\n\n## Quick Reference\n\n**Navigation codes** (for grep):\n- `QST:` Questions\n- `ANS:` Answers\n- `COD:` Code examples\n- `FIL:` Files to examine\n- `DOC:` Documentation\n\n---\n\n## Context\n\nThe Architecture of Complexity skill needs to be packaged in a format that:\n1. Works with Claude Code's skill system\n2. Follows the Agent Skills open standard\n3. Can be distributed as a single `.skill` file\n4. Includes proper metadata for discovery and invocation\n\nFrom CLAUDE.md open questions:\n- How to write the license correctly?\n- How to link the CC license to the right content?\n- What frontmatter to add to the SKILL.md so it will compile?\n\n---\n\n## Research Conducted\n\n### FIL: Documentation examined\n- `/docs/resources/extend-claude-with-skills.md` - Official Anthropic skill documentation\n- `/docs/resources/visual-diffusion.skill` - Working example skill (unpacked and analyzed)\n- `/docs/resources/agentskills/` - Agent Skills standard specification\n\n### COD: Visual Diffusion Skill Structure\n```\nvisual-diffusion.skill (ZIP file)\n\u2514\u2500\u2500 visual-diffusion/\n    \u251c\u2500\u2500 SKILL.md (with YAML frontmatter)\n    \u2514\u2500\u2500 references/\n        \u251c\u2500\u2500 image-prompting.md\n        \u251c\u2500\u2500 model-taxonomy.md\n        \u251c\u2500\u2500 quick-reference.md\n        \u2514\u2500\u2500 video-prompting.md\n```\n\n### COD: Frontmatter Format (from visual-diffusion/SKILL.md)\n```yaml\n---\nname: visual-diffusion\ndescription: Expert prompting for image and video diffusion models (Nano Banana, GPT Image 1, DALL-E 3, Midjourney, Stable Diffusion, FLUX, Sora, Runway, Veo, Kling). Use when creating high-fidelity visual content - album art, concept art, creative visuals - where technical vocabulary and model-specific syntax dramatically improve output quality. Specializes in iterative dialogic refinement, multi-concept generation, and model-specific optimization.\n---\n\n# Visual Diffusion Model Prompting\n\n[Markdown content follows...]\n```\n\n---\n\n## Decision\n\n### Packaging Format: ZIP Archive\n\n**Chosen**: ZIP file containing skill directory with proper structure\n\n**Structure**:\n```\narchitecture-of-complexity.skill (ZIP file)\n\u2514\u2500\u2500 architecture-of-complexity/\n    \u251c\u2500\u2500 SKILL.md (required - with YAML frontmatter)\n    \u251c\u2500\u2500 README.md\n    \u251c\u2500\u2500 INSTALLATION.md\n    \u251c\u2500\u2500 META-ARCHITECTURE.md\n    \u2514\u2500\u2500 resources/\n        \u251c\u2500\u2500 simon_1962_full.txt\n        \u251c\u2500\u2500 simon_architecture_summary.md\n        \u2514\u2500\u2500 examples/\n            \u251c\u2500\u2500 cam.md\n            \u251c\u2500\u2500 mcp-design.md\n            \u2514\u2500\u2500 vue-react.md\n```\n\n**Why**:\n- Matches Claude Code's expected format\n- Follows Agent Skills standard\n- Enables single-file distribution\n- Supports directory of supporting files\n\n**Trade-offs accepted**:\n- Slightly more complex than single file\n- Requires build step to create ZIP\n- Users must unzip to modify\n\n---\n\n### Frontmatter Schema: YAML at Top\n\n**Chosen**: YAML frontmatter between `---` markers at the very beginning of SKILL.md\n\n**Required fields**:\n```yaml\n---\nname: architecture-of-complexity\ndescription: [One-line description that helps Claude decide when to load the skill]\n---\n```\n\n**Optional fields we'll use**:\n- `disable-model-invocation: false` (default - allow Claude to auto-invoke)\n- `user-invocable: true` (default - show in `/` menu)\n\n**Why**:\n- Matches Anthropic specification exactly\n- Enables Claude's automatic skill discovery\n- Description drives when skill is loaded\n- Name becomes the `/architecture-of-complexity` command\n\n**Current Problem in SKILL.md**:\n```markdown\n# Architecture of Complexity Skill\n\n**Authors:** J\u00e9r\u00e9mie Lumbroso and Claude Sonnet 4.5\n**License:** [CC BY-SA 4.0](...)\n**Version:** 1.0 (February 2026)\n\n**Note:** CAM framework is Copyright \u00a9 J\u00e9r\u00e9mie Lumbroso\n\n---\n\n## When to Use This Skill\n[...]\n```\n\nThis is **WRONG** because:\n1. Frontmatter must come BEFORE any markdown content\n2. Frontmatter must be YAML, not markdown\n3. The `---` separator is currently in the wrong place\n\n**Correct Format**:\n```yaml\n---\nname: architecture-of-complexity\ndescription: Apply Herbert Simon's Architecture of Complexity principles to system design, code architecture, and UI decisions. Use for understanding hierarchical decomposition, near-decomposability, stable intermediate forms, and architectural trade-offs. Helps analyze modularity, coupling, and complexity in software systems.\n---\n\n# Architecture of Complexity Skill\n\n**Authors:** J\u00e9r\u00e9mie Lumbroso and Claude Sonnet 4.5\n**License:** [CC BY-SA 4.0](https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/)\n**Version:** 1.0.0 (February 2026)\n\n**Note:** The CAM (Computationally-Assisted Metacognition) framework and Third X (LLM output linearization framework) referenced herein are Copyright \u00a9 J\u00e9r\u00e9mie Lumbroso. This skill document itself is licensed under CC BY-SA 4.0.\n\n---\n\n## When to Use This Skill\n[...]\n```\n\n---\n\n## Consequences\n\n### Immediate Actions Needed\n1. Fix SKILL.md to have proper YAML frontmatter at top\n2. Move attribution/license info below frontmatter (but keep it visible)\n3. Create build script that ZIPs the skill directory\n4. Test that Claude Code can load the skill correctly\n\n### Long-term Benefits\n- Skill will be auto-discovered by Claude when relevant\n- Users can invoke with `/architecture-of-complexity`\n- Distribution as single .skill file\n- Follows standard that other AI tools can adopt\n\n### Documentation Impact\n- README and INSTALLATION should reference the frontmatter\n- CI/CD must validate frontmatter syntax\n- Release process creates properly formatted ZIP\n\n---\n\n## Action Items\n\n- [x] Document packaging format decision - Owner: Claude Code\n- [x] Document frontmatter schema decision - Owner: Claude Code\n- [ ] Fix SKILL.md frontmatter - Owner: Claude Code (next task)\n- [ ] Create build/packaging script - Owner: Claude Code (CI/CD task)\n- [ ] Validate skill loads correctly - Owner: Both (testing)\n\n---\n\n## Supporting Materials\n\n### DOC: Anthropic Skill Specification\n\nFrom extend-claude-with-skills.md:\n\n> Every skill needs a `SKILL.md` file with two parts: YAML frontmatter (between `---` markers) that tells Claude when to use the skill, and markdown content with instructions Claude follows when the skill is invoked. The `name` field becomes the `/slash-command`, and the `description` helps Claude decide when to load it automatically.\n\nKey fields:\n- **name** (optional, defaults to directory name): Lowercase, hyphens, max 64 chars\n- **description** (recommended): What the skill does and when to use it\n- **disable-model-invocation** (optional, default false): Set true to prevent auto-invoke\n- **user-invocable** (optional, default true): Set false to hide from menu\n\n### DOC: File Size Recommendations\n\nFrom extend-claude-with-skills.md:\n\n> Keep `SKILL.md` under 500 lines. Move detailed reference material to separate files.\n\n**Current status**:\n- SKILL.md: 408 lines \u2705 (under limit)\n- Supporting files properly separated \u2705\n\n---\n\n## Validation\n\n- [x] Human: Decision matches Claude Code specification\n- [x] AI: Understood the requirements from documentation\n- [x] Verified against working example (visual-diffusion.skill)\n\n**Notes**: This ADR establishes the foundational packaging format. Subsequent ADRs will cover versioning (ADR-0002) and licensing (ADR-0003).\n\n---\n\n## Iterations\n\n### Iteration 1 (2026-02-27)\n- Human: Provided extend-claude-with-skills.md documentation\n- Human: Provided visual-diffusion.skill as working example\n- AI: Analyzed both sources, identified current SKILL.md formatting problem\n- AI: Documented correct format with specific frontmatter schema\n- Outcome: Clear specification for fixing SKILL.md and packaging skill\n\n---\n\n## Links\n\n- Related: ADR-0002 (Versioning Strategy) - to be created\n- Related: ADR-0003 (License File Structure) - to be created\n- Reference: `/docs/resources/extend-claude-with-skills.md`\n- Example: `/docs/resources/visual-diffusion.skill`\n"
}

{
  "file_path": "/Users/jlumbroso/Programming/claude/architecture-of-complexity-skill/docs/adr/0002-versioning-strategy.md",
  "content": "# ADR-0002: Versioning Strategy\n\n- **Date**: 2026-02-27\n- **Iteration**: 1\n- **Status**: Accepted\n- **Deciders**: J\u00e9r\u00e9mie Lumbroso, Claude Code Sonnet 4.5\n\n---\n\n## Context\n\nThe skill needs a versioning system that:\n1. Communicates compatibility and changes clearly\n2. Enables automated release management\n3. Works well with Git tags and GitHub Releases\n4. Follows community standards\n\nFrom conversation:\n> **J\u00e9r\u00e9mie**: \"The versioning preference, I said Semver as you recommended\"\n\n---\n\n## Decision\n\n### Chosen: Semantic Versioning (SemVer)\n\n**Format**: `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` (e.g., `1.0.0`, `1.1.0`, `2.0.0`)\n\n**Increment rules for this skill**:\n\n- **MAJOR**: Breaking changes to skill principles or framework\n  - Example: Fundamentally different interpretation of Simon's architecture\n  - Example: Removing core concepts (near-decomposability, etc.)\n  - Example: Changing skill invocation behavior in incompatible ways\n\n- **MINOR**: New examples, enhanced explanations, additional concepts\n  - Example: Adding new example (e.g., database-design.md)\n  - Example: Expanding existing sections with more detail\n  - Example: Adding new but related principles from complexity theory\n\n- **PATCH**: Fixes, typos, clarifications, formatting\n  - Example: Fixing typos or grammatical errors\n  - Example: Improving code examples for clarity\n  - Example: Better formatting of markdown\n\n**Initial version**: `1.0.0`\n- Rationale: Skill is complete and functional, ready for production use\n\n---\n\n## Alternatives Considered\n\n### Option A: Date-Based Versioning (2026.02.27)\n- **Pros**: Clear temporal ordering, simple to generate\n- **Cons**: No semantic information about changes, harder to assess compatibility\n- **Verdict**: Less suitable for a teaching skill where content stability matters\n\n### Option B: Hybrid (1.0-20260227)\n- **Pros**: Both semantic and temporal information\n- **Cons**: More complex, non-standard format\n- **Verdict**: Unnecessary complexity for our use case\n\n### Option C: Single Number (v1, v2, v3)\n- **Pros**: Extremely simple\n- **Cons**: No granularity for minor updates vs breaking changes\n- **Verdict**: Too coarse-grained\n\n---\n\n## Implementation\n\n### Where Version Appears\n\n1. **SKILL.md frontmatter** (metadata):\n   ```markdown\n   **Version:** 1.0.0\n   ```\n\n2. **Git tags** (for releases):\n   ```bash\n   git tag v1.0.0\n   git tag v1.1.0\n   ```\n\n3. **GitHub Releases** (automatically from tags):\n   - Release title: `v1.0.0 - Architecture of Complexity Skill`\n   - Asset name: `architecture-of-complexity-1.0.0.skill`\n\n4. **ZIP filename** (packaged skill):\n   - Format: `architecture-of-complexity-{version}.skill`\n   - Example: `architecture-of-complexity-1.0.0.skill`\n\n### Version Bumping Process\n\n**Manual (initial approach)**:\n1. Decide change type (major/minor/patch)\n2. Update version in SKILL.md\n3. Commit: `chore: bump version to X.Y.Z`\n4. Tag: `git tag vX.Y.Z`\n5. Push: `git push && git push --tags`\n6. CI creates release automatically\n\n**Future automation options**:\n- Conventional commits + auto-bump\n- Manual workflow dispatch with version input\n- Not implementing now - YAGNI\n\n---\n\n## Consequences\n\n### Benefits\n- Clear compatibility signaling\n- Standard format (widely understood)\n- Works well with Git tags and GitHub Releases\n- CI/CD can extract version from tag (`v1.0.0` \u2192 `1.0.0`)\n\n### Trade-offs\n- Requires discipline to follow increment rules\n- Manual version bumping (for now)\n- Need to document versioning semantics for contributors\n\n### Examples of Future Versions\n\n**v1.0.0** \u2192 Initial release (current)\n**v1.0.1** \u2192 Fix typo in watchmaker parable explanation\n**v1.1.0** \u2192 Add new example: kubernetes-architecture.md\n**v1.2.0** \u2192 Expand \"Empty World Hypothesis\" section with more detail\n**v2.0.0** \u2192 Restructure around different organizational framework (unlikely)\n\n---\n\n## Action Items\n\n- [x] Decide on SemVer format - Owner: Both\n- [ ] Update SKILL.md to version 1.0.0 - Owner: Claude Code\n- [ ] Document versioning rules in CONTRIBUTING.md - Owner: Claude Code\n- [ ] Configure CI/CD to use version from git tags - Owner: Claude Code\n\n---\n\n## Validation\n\n- [x] Human: Agreed to SemVer\n- [x] AI: Confirmed suitability for teaching/reference skill\n\n---\n\n## Supporting Materials\n\n### NOT: Versioning Semantics for This Skill\n\nTeaching skills are different from code:\n- **Breaking change** means: incompatible interpretation of theory\n- **Minor change** means: enhanced understanding, new examples\n- **Patch** means: corrections, clarifications\n\nThis differs from API versioning where MAJOR = breaking API changes.\n\n### NOT: Git Tag Format\n\nUse `v` prefix for tags:\n- \u2705 `v1.0.0`, `v1.1.0`, `v2.0.0`\n- \u274c `1.0.0` (no prefix)\n- \u274c `release-1.0.0` (too verbose)\n\nRationale: Standard Git convention, CI parsing is easier\n\n---\n\n## Iterations\n\n### Iteration 1 (2026-02-27)\n- Human: Expressed preference for SemVer based on AI recommendation\n- AI: Analyzed options, confirmed SemVer suitability\n- AI: Defined increment rules specific to teaching skill\n- AI: Specified implementation locations\n- Outcome: SemVer adopted with initial version 1.0.0\n\n---\n\n## Links\n\n- Supersedes: Initial conversation preference for SemVer\n- Related: ADR-0001 (Skill Packaging Format)\n- Related: ADR-0003 (License File Structure) - to be created\n- Reference: [Semantic Versioning](https://semver.org/)\n"
}
```
